The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.


5. Integrating the records without exaggeration

The records reveal several recurring needs. Reliable operation requires clear responsibilities after the contractor leaves. Energy results must be interpreted alongside service hours and equipment faults. Visitor experience depends on access, noise, and the ability to use the building, not just a comfortable average temperature. Evidence improves when operators preserve original observations, explain corrections, and document where sensors were placed. None of these needs can be reduced to installing a new dashboard.

The three rounds of field visits are not three independent replications of the energy study. Inspectors returned to related locations and often investigated earlier concerns. It would be incorrect to count the thirty-six case notes as thirty-six independent renovated libraries. The pilot still consists of twelve renovated libraries and twelve comparison libraries. Keeping that denominator clear is essential when translating a detailed operational report into a short public summary.

Some recommendations were adopted during the monitoring period, which complicates evaluation. If a ventilation schedule changed halfway through a month, the month does not represent a single stable operating condition. The team retained dates of adjustments and planned sensitivity analyses. It did not discard every difficult month, because doing so would selectively remove the practical problems that expansion would also encounter.

Comparing energy use per visitor can be informative, but the report advises against treating that ratio as a universal target. A quiet room for vulnerable readers may serve an important function without high footfall. A crowded event may have a low energy cost per attendee while creating accessibility problems. Decisions require a statement of the public service being protected. Efficiency is not a substitute for specifying the purpose of a library.

The study also distinguishes electricity from total environmental impact. Materials used in renovation have production and disposal consequences. Solar equipment requires maintenance. Trees need space and water before they mature. The pilot did not measure every lifecycle impact. Its results should not be described as a complete carbon assessment, and the council should not promise a quantified citywide emissions reduction on the basis of these meters alone.

6. Proposed next phase

The next phase should retain a comparison group and, where feasible, use a randomised order of renovation among eligible sites. Eligibility rules should be published before recruitment. Sites with urgent safety needs should receive repairs without being forced into an experiment, but their results should be reported separately. This approach recognises ethical obligations while reducing the risk that enthusiastic volunteers alone determine the apparent success of the programme.

The evaluation team proposes publishing monthly measurements with documented corrections, including missing-data flags and changes in opening hours. Energy demand, electricity purchases, and solar generation should remain separate. A short narrative should accompany unusual periods, such as construction closures or equipment failures. Publication should include unsuccessful interventions and additional maintenance costs, not only photographs of completed work.

Survey recruitment should reach people who stopped visiting as well as current visitors. Accessible formats and varied collection times may improve coverage, but a higher response count does not automatically remove selection bias. The next report should show who was invited, who responded, and which questions were left unanswered. Qualitative interviews can explain barriers that standard scales miss, provided their limits are made explicit.

A lifecycle cost review is also required before a large expansion. The review should include routine maintenance, expected replacement, staff training, and downtime. It should examine what happens when a warranty expires and whether spare parts remain available. The city should not rank proposals by initial purchase cost alone, nor assume that every more expensive option will eventually save money.

The recommendation is a controlled, documented expansion with staged decisions, not an immediate citywide rollout. Before each stage, managers should verify accessible routes, working measurement equipment, a funded maintenance plan, and a clear arrangement for staff support. The council should be willing to stop or revise an intervention when it reduces service quality even if a narrow energy indicator improves.

7. Final conclusion

The pilot offers encouraging observations: purchased electricity fell more in renovated libraries than in the comparison group, many survey respondents preferred the indoor environment, and several operational changes improved daily use. These findings justify further evaluation. They do not prove a universal causal saving, universal public approval, or financial payback.

A responsible summary should preserve the central distinction. Fourteen percent and six percent describe the two observed reductions; the eight-percentage-point difference is not a proven treatment effect. Survey preference concerns respondents, and the spending total does not capture lifecycle costs. Continued comparison, better documentation, representative feedback, and realistic maintenance planning are the next priorities.

END OF ARTICLE


VOLUME 04 OF 12 — REFERENCE COPY

ARTICLE: The Riverhaven Public Buildings Pilot
A fictional evaluation report for reading-comprehension and long-context testing

1. Purpose and decision

Riverhaven is a fictional city whose public buildings face hot summers, unreliable maintenance schedules, and uneven access to quiet indoor space. The city council approved a pilot to renovate public libraries and measure the consequences before expanding the programme. The study concerns electricity, indoor comfort, operating costs, and access. It is not a trial of every environmental policy in the city. All names, dates, institutions, and quantities in this document are invented. The distinction matters because the report describes an evaluation design, not evidence about a real community.

The main intervention combined roof insulation, external shade, improved ventilation controls, and staff training. Some sites also tested solar panels, but solar generation was recorded separately from electricity demand. The council wanted to avoid claiming that a building used less energy merely because a new panel supplied part of the same demand. Tree planting formed a smaller demonstration component outside selected entrances. Newly planted trees did not provide the canopy of mature trees, so the report does not attribute immediate indoor savings to those new trees.

Twelve libraries entered the renovation group. Twelve other libraries were selected as comparison sites on the basis of floor area, opening hours, and the age of the cooling system. Selection was not random. Managers volunteered, and buildings with urgent structural damage were excluded. The two groups therefore cannot be assumed identical. In particular, managers who volunteered may already have been more interested in energy management. That difference could influence electricity use even without physical renovation.

The monitoring period covered twelve months before and twelve months after completion. The primary descriptive result was a fourteen percent reduction in purchased electricity in renovated libraries. The comparison libraries recorded a six percent reduction over the same period. The gap is eight percentage points, not proof of an eight percent causal effect. Weather, attendance, opening hours, equipment replacement, and differences in management all remain possible explanations for part of the gap. The report does not provide a randomised estimate.

2. What the measurements can and cannot show

Monthly meters provide a more reliable total than a visitor survey, but meter data do not explain every change in that total. A broken cooling unit can reduce electricity consumption while making conditions worse. A library that closes early can look efficient while serving fewer people. For that reason, the team retained records of service hours, cancellations, complaints, and occupancy. It also logged equipment faults. Results were reviewed alongside those records rather than ranked by electricity use alone.

Summer savings were larger than winter savings. The report does not convert that pattern into a forecast for every future year. One summer was cooler than the previous summer, and winter attendance changed when several schools altered their timetables. Indoor sensors showed improved afternoon comfort at many renovated sites, but the measurements were incomplete. A sensor placed next to a shaded wall is not representative of the entire reading room. Staff moved some sensors during cleaning; those periods were marked rather than silently treated as comparable.

The survey found that sixty-eight percent of responding visitors preferred the renovated indoor environment. Only forty-one percent of invited visitors completed the questionnaire. Preference among respondents is therefore not equivalent to approval among all residents. People who stopped visiting were especially difficult to reach. The report records this limitation in its main conclusions, not only in an appendix. Comments about quiet space, accessibility, and delayed construction are considered useful explanations, not direct substitutes for representative survey evidence.

The approved budget was twelve million fictional currency units, and recorded expenditure was eleven point four million. That difference is an accounting observation, not a lifecycle benefit calculation. Future maintenance, replacement parts, staff time, and the possible disposal of old equipment have not all been observed. The report does not claim that the project has paid for itself. Several contractors offered warranties with different coverage, making direct comparisons of initial purchase prices misleading.

3. Implementation, access, and mixed outcomes

Construction did not proceed uniformly. Two sites experienced delivery delays that pushed work into examination season. One site kept its main reading room open but temporarily closed a step-free entrance. The team treated that as an accessibility failure requiring correction, even though the electricity meter continued to show lower consumption. A low energy bill cannot compensate for excluding readers who need the accessible entrance.

Staff training helped operators understand timers and ventilation settings, but turnover reduced continuity. A simple guide near the control panel proved useful at several sites. Elsewhere, staff avoided changing unfamiliar settings for fear of damaging equipment. The team therefore recommended a support arrangement with named responsibilities rather than relying on a single training session. Training attendance does not by itself demonstrate that a control system was used correctly in practice.

Residents appreciated quieter streets during periods without construction traffic and liked existing shade near entrances. Shopkeepers reported that temporary barriers disrupted deliveries. Those observations are not contradictory: groups used the area differently and at different times. The evaluation records both. It does not average incompatible experiences into a single unqualified claim that all residents benefited.

Several small interventions did not work as expected. A ventilation schedule at one library conflicted with an evening study session. A demonstration rainwater tank required more cleaning than anticipated. A dashboard made electricity trends visible but confused some users by mixing daily and monthly scales. The report includes these unsuccessful trials because excluding them would overstate how easily the programme could be expanded.

4. How to read the field records

The following records describe repeated monitoring visits across different service zones. A service zone is an administrative grouping for organising visits, not an additional experimental group. Each record is a qualitative case note. The records deliberately include different kinds of evidence: meter observations, staff explanations, visitor accounts, and suggested changes. They are not independent measurements of the citywide reduction reported above.

A district may appear in more than one operational discussion, and the same type of building may appear in several districts. The visits were scheduled to investigate problems, so a high number of critical notes does not establish a population prevalence. Conversely, a positive note does not establish that the same result holds at unvisited sites. The intended reading task is to identify recurring mechanisms and limitations while keeping the overall study design in view.

Inspectors used the existing maintenance log before proposing a causal explanation. Where records were missing, they recorded uncertainty instead of filling the gap with a plausible story. They preferred repairs that preserved public access and could be checked with the available equipment. A proposal became a recommendation for further testing only after someone was assigned to implement it and document the outcome. None of the notes below changes the main statistical conclusion.

Field record 01: Service zone 1, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined afternoon cooling in the central reading room. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Visitors chose seats near the shaded wall, while seats beside the west-facing glass remained less popular. Seat choice also depended on power sockets and proximity to the reference desk, so it was not a temperature measurement. The proposed next action was to move a calibrated sensor through several seating zones and compare occupied hours rather than a single daily reading. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 02: Service zone 2, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined ventilation timers in the evening study area. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. The ventilation timer stopped before a newly extended study session ended, and staff opened windows to compensate. Outdoor noise then changed the use of the room, so a simple before-and-after satisfaction score mixed two different effects. The proposed next action was to align the timer with the published timetable, record manual overrides, and check air quality during late sessions. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 03: Service zone 3, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined construction barriers in the accessible entrance. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A temporary materials enclosure narrowed the accessible route and forced some visitors to ask staff for assistance. A lower visitor count during that week could reflect the obstruction rather than reduced demand for library services. The proposed next action was to relocate the enclosure, preserve the required route width, and invite accessibility representatives to inspect the change. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 04: Service zone 4, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined solar metering in the roof services area. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. The dashboard displayed imported electricity but omitted locally generated electricity used inside the building. Reading that display alone would confuse lower purchases with lower demand and exaggerate the effect of insulation. The proposed next action was to publish demand and generation as separate series with clear units and preserve the original meter readings. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 05: Service zone 5, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined acoustic comfort in the children's section. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Teachers valued quieter group sessions after a noisy fan was repaired, but weekend visitors reported little change. The groups attended at different times and used different rooms, so their responses were not directly comparable. The proposed next action was to record the room and time for each survey response and compare like sessions before drawing broader conclusions. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 06: Service zone 6, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined tree maintenance in the shaded courtyard. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Existing mature trees provided useful shade, while newly planted trees required watering and protected soil beds. The immediate comfort benefit could not be attributed entirely to new planting because the mature canopy predated the project. The proposed next action was to separate established and newly planted canopy in the records and track survival over several growing seasons. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 07: Service zone 7, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined operator training in the equipment cupboard. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A substitute caretaker could not find the control guide and left an unfamiliar setting unchanged for the weekend. The presence of a completed training attendance sheet did not demonstrate that every later operator had usable instructions. The proposed next action was to place an updated guide beside the panel, nominate backup staff, and test whether a new operator can follow it. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 08: Service zone 8, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined occupancy changes in the community meeting room. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A neighbourhood club moved its meetings into the library, increasing evening attendance and equipment use. More electricity during those hours might accompany a larger public service rather than indicate renovation failure. The proposed next action was to report opening hours and occupied room hours alongside energy totals without concealing the absolute consumption. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 09: Service zone 9, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined rainwater storage in the water collection yard. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A small demonstration tank needed additional cleaning after leaves blocked a screen during a wet week. A short successful demonstration did not establish annual maintenance costs or guarantee that stored water was suitable for every use. The proposed next action was to record cleaning labour, label permitted uses, and compare the actual workload with the original maintenance plan. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 10: Service zone 10, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined energy dashboards in the public information desk. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A display placed daily values beside monthly totals without prominent unit labels, leading readers to infer a sudden increase. Confusion about chart scales was not evidence of an actual change in electricity consumption. The proposed next action was to use consistent intervals, state units on every chart, and ask ordinary visitors to explain a sample trend. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 11: Service zone 11, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined local business disruption in the delivery entrance. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Shopkeepers reported missed delivery slots while construction vehicles occupied the shared loading area. The complaint described a real local cost but did not measure the total financial effect on every business in the neighbourhood. The proposed next action was to agree delivery windows in advance, log conflicts, and include affected traders when reviewing the construction schedule. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 12: Service zone 12, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined sensor placement in the archive room. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A temperature sensor was moved beside an internal wall during shelf maintenance and later returned to its original bracket. The resulting discontinuity could be mistaken for improved building performance if the move was omitted from the dataset. The proposed next action was to mark the relocation interval, retain both raw series, and repeat the measurement with documented placement. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 36: Service zone 12, seasonal review

During the seasonal review, the team examined sensor placement in the archive room. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. A temperature sensor was moved beside an internal wall during shelf maintenance and later returned to its original bracket. The resulting discontinuity could be mistaken for improved building performance if the move was omitted from the dataset. The proposed next action was to mark the relocation interval, retain both raw series, and repeat the measurement with documented placement. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.


5. Integrating the records without exaggeration

The records reveal several recurring needs. Reliable operation requires clear responsibilities after the contractor leaves. Energy results must be interpreted alongside service hours and equipment faults. Visitor experience depends on access, noise, and the ability to use the building, not just a comfortable average temperature. Evidence improves when operators preserve original observations, explain corrections, and document where sensors were placed. None of these needs can be reduced to installing a new dashboard.

The three rounds of field visits are not three independent replications of the energy study. Inspectors returned to related locations and often investigated earlier concerns. It would be incorrect to count the thirty-six case notes as thirty-six independent renovated libraries. The pilot still consists of twelve renovated libraries and twelve comparison libraries. Keeping that denominator clear is essential when translating a detailed operational report into a short public summary.

Some recommendations were adopted during the monitoring period, which complicates evaluation. If a ventilation schedule changed halfway through a month, the month does not represent a single stable operating condition. The team retained dates of adjustments and planned sensitivity analyses. It did not discard every difficult month, because doing so would selectively remove the practical problems that expansion would also encounter.

Comparing energy use per visitor can be informative, but the report advises against treating that ratio as a universal target. A quiet room for vulnerable readers may serve an important function without high footfall. A crowded event may have a low energy cost per attendee while creating accessibility problems. Decisions require a statement of the public service being protected. Efficiency is not a substitute for specifying the purpose of a library.

The study also distinguishes electricity from total environmental impact. Materials used in renovation have production and disposal consequences. Solar equipment requires maintenance. Trees need space and water before they mature. The pilot did not measure every lifecycle impact. Its results should not be described as a complete carbon assessment, and the council should not promise a quantified citywide emissions reduction on the basis of these meters alone.

6. Proposed next phase

The next phase should retain a comparison group and, where feasible, use a randomised order of renovation among eligible sites. Eligibility rules should be published before recruitment. Sites with urgent safety needs should receive repairs without being forced into an experiment, but their results should be reported separately. This approach recognises ethical obligations while reducing the risk that enthusiastic volunteers alone determine the apparent success of the programme.

The evaluation team proposes publishing monthly measurements with documented corrections, including missing-data flags and changes in opening hours. Energy demand, electricity purchases, and solar generation should remain separate. A short narrative should accompany unusual periods, such as construction closures or equipment failures. Publication should include unsuccessful interventions and additional maintenance costs, not only photographs of completed work.

Survey recruitment should reach people who stopped visiting as well as current visitors. Accessible formats and varied collection times may improve coverage, but a higher response count does not automatically remove selection bias. The next report should show who was invited, who responded, and which questions were left unanswered. Qualitative interviews can explain barriers that standard scales miss, provided their limits are made explicit.

A lifecycle cost review is also required before a large expansion. The review should include routine maintenance, expected replacement, staff training, and downtime. It should examine what happens when a warranty expires and whether spare parts remain available. The city should not rank proposals by initial purchase cost alone, nor assume that every more expensive option will eventually save money.

The recommendation is a controlled, documented expansion with staged decisions, not an immediate citywide rollout. Before each stage, managers should verify accessible routes, working measurement equipment, a funded maintenance plan, and a clear arrangement for staff support. The council should be willing to stop or revise an intervention when it reduces service quality even if a narrow energy indicator improves.

7. Final conclusion

The pilot offers encouraging observations: purchased electricity fell more in renovated libraries than in the comparison group, many survey respondents preferred the indoor environment, and several operational changes improved daily use. These findings justify further evaluation. They do not prove a universal causal saving, universal public approval, or financial payback.

A responsible summary should preserve the central distinction. Fourteen percent and six percent describe the two observed reductions; the eight-percentage-point difference is not a proven treatment effect. Survey preference concerns respondents, and the spending total does not capture lifecycle costs. Continued comparison, better documentation, representative feedback, and realistic maintenance planning are the next priorities.

END OF ARTICLE


VOLUME 05 OF 12 — REFERENCE COPY

ARTICLE: The Riverhaven Public Buildings Pilot
A fictional evaluation report for reading-comprehension and long-context testing

1. Purpose and decision

Riverhaven is a fictional city whose public buildings face hot summers, unreliable maintenance schedules, and uneven access to quiet indoor space. The city council approved a pilot to renovate public libraries and measure the consequences before expanding the programme. The study concerns electricity, indoor comfort, operating costs, and access. It is not a trial of every environmental policy in the city. All names, dates, institutions, and quantities in this document are invented. The distinction matters because the report describes an evaluation design, not evidence about a real community.

The main intervention combined roof insulation, external shade, improved ventilation controls, and staff training. Some sites also tested solar panels, but solar generation was recorded separately from electricity demand. The council wanted to avoid claiming that a building used less energy merely because a new panel supplied part of the same demand. Tree planting formed a smaller demonstration component outside selected entrances. Newly planted trees did not provide the canopy of mature trees, so the report does not attribute immediate indoor savings to those new trees.

Twelve libraries entered the renovation group. Twelve other libraries were selected as comparison sites on the basis of floor area, opening hours, and the age of the cooling system. Selection was not random. Managers volunteered, and buildings with urgent structural damage were excluded. The two groups therefore cannot be assumed identical. In particular, managers who volunteered may already have been more interested in energy management. That difference could influence electricity use even without physical renovation.

The monitoring period covered twelve months before and twelve months after completion. The primary descriptive result was a fourteen percent reduction in purchased electricity in renovated libraries. The comparison libraries recorded a six percent reduction over the same period. The gap is eight percentage points, not proof of an eight percent causal effect. Weather, attendance, opening hours, equipment replacement, and differences in management all remain possible explanations for part of the gap. The report does not provide a randomised estimate.

2. What the measurements can and cannot show

Monthly meters provide a more reliable total than a visitor survey, but meter data do not explain every change in that total. A broken cooling unit can reduce electricity consumption while making conditions worse. A library that closes early can look efficient while serving fewer people. For that reason, the team retained records of service hours, cancellations, complaints, and occupancy. It also logged equipment faults. Results were reviewed alongside those records rather than ranked by electricity use alone.

Summer savings were larger than winter savings. The report does not convert that pattern into a forecast for every future year. One summer was cooler than the previous summer, and winter attendance changed when several schools altered their timetables. Indoor sensors showed improved afternoon comfort at many renovated sites, but the measurements were incomplete. A sensor placed next to a shaded wall is not representative of the entire reading room. Staff moved some sensors during cleaning; those periods were marked rather than silently treated as comparable.

The survey found that sixty-eight percent of responding visitors preferred the renovated indoor environment. Only forty-one percent of invited visitors completed the questionnaire. Preference among respondents is therefore not equivalent to approval among all residents. People who stopped visiting were especially difficult to reach. The report records this limitation in its main conclusions, not only in an appendix. Comments about quiet space, accessibility, and delayed construction are considered useful explanations, not direct substitutes for representative survey evidence.

The approved budget was twelve million fictional currency units, and recorded expenditure was eleven point four million. That difference is an accounting observation, not a lifecycle benefit calculation. Future maintenance, replacement parts, staff time, and the possible disposal of old equipment have not all been observed. The report does not claim that the project has paid for itself. Several contractors offered warranties with different coverage, making direct comparisons of initial purchase prices misleading.

3. Implementation, access, and mixed outcomes

Construction did not proceed uniformly. Two sites experienced delivery delays that pushed work into examination season. One site kept its main reading room open but temporarily closed a step-free entrance. The team treated that as an accessibility failure requiring correction, even though the electricity meter continued to show lower consumption. A low energy bill cannot compensate for excluding readers who need the accessible entrance.

Staff training helped operators understand timers and ventilation settings, but turnover reduced continuity. A simple guide near the control panel proved useful at several sites. Elsewhere, staff avoided changing unfamiliar settings for fear of damaging equipment. The team therefore recommended a support arrangement with named responsibilities rather than relying on a single training session. Training attendance does not by itself demonstrate that a control system was used correctly in practice.

Residents appreciated quieter streets during periods without construction traffic and liked existing shade near entrances. Shopkeepers reported that temporary barriers disrupted deliveries. Those observations are not contradictory: groups used the area differently and at different times. The evaluation records both. It does not average incompatible experiences into a single unqualified claim that all residents benefited.

Several small interventions did not work as expected. A ventilation schedule at one library conflicted with an evening study session. A demonstration rainwater tank required more cleaning than anticipated. A dashboard made electricity trends visible but confused some users by mixing daily and monthly scales. The report includes these unsuccessful trials because excluding them would overstate how easily the programme could be expanded.

4. How to read the field records

The following records describe repeated monitoring visits across different service zones. A service zone is an administrative grouping for organising visits, not an additional experimental group. Each record is a qualitative case note. The records deliberately include different kinds of evidence: meter observations, staff explanations, visitor accounts, and suggested changes. They are not independent measurements of the citywide reduction reported above.

A district may appear in more than one operational discussion, and the same type of building may appear in several districts. The visits were scheduled to investigate problems, so a high number of critical notes does not establish a population prevalence. Conversely, a positive note does not establish that the same result holds at unvisited sites. The intended reading task is to identify recurring mechanisms and limitations while keeping the overall study design in view.

Inspectors used the existing maintenance log before proposing a causal explanation. Where records were missing, they recorded uncertainty instead of filling the gap with a plausible story. They preferred repairs that preserved public access and could be checked with the available equipment. A proposal became a recommendation for further testing only after someone was assigned to implement it and document the outcome. None of the notes below changes the main statistical conclusion.

Field record 01: Service zone 1, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined afternoon cooling in the central reading room. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Visitors chose seats near the shaded wall, while seats beside the west-facing glass remained less popular. Seat choice also depended on power sockets and proximity to the reference desk, so it was not a temperature measurement. The proposed next action was to move a calibrated sensor through several seating zones and compare occupied hours rather than a single daily reading. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 02: Service zone 2, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined ventilation timers in the evening study area. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. The ventilation timer stopped before a newly extended study session ended, and staff opened windows to compensate. Outdoor noise then changed the use of the room, so a simple before-and-after satisfaction score mixed two different effects. The proposed next action was to align the timer with the published timetable, record manual overrides, and check air quality during late sessions. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 03: Service zone 3, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined construction barriers in the accessible entrance. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A temporary materials enclosure narrowed the accessible route and forced some visitors to ask staff for assistance. A lower visitor count during that week could reflect the obstruction rather than reduced demand for library services. The proposed next action was to relocate the enclosure, preserve the required route width, and invite accessibility representatives to inspect the change. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 04: Service zone 4, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined solar metering in the roof services area. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. The dashboard displayed imported electricity but omitted locally generated electricity used inside the building. Reading that display alone would confuse lower purchases with lower demand and exaggerate the effect of insulation. The proposed next action was to publish demand and generation as separate series with clear units and preserve the original meter readings. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 05: Service zone 5, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined acoustic comfort in the children's section. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Teachers valued quieter group sessions after a noisy fan was repaired, but weekend visitors reported little change. The groups attended at different times and used different rooms, so their responses were not directly comparable. The proposed next action was to record the room and time for each survey response and compare like sessions before drawing broader conclusions. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 06: Service zone 6, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined tree maintenance in the shaded courtyard. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Existing mature trees provided useful shade, while newly planted trees required watering and protected soil beds. The immediate comfort benefit could not be attributed entirely to new planting because the mature canopy predated the project. The proposed next action was to separate established and newly planted canopy in the records and track survival over several growing seasons. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. Field record 07: Service zone 7, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined operator training in the equipment cupboard. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. A substitute caretaker could not find the control guide and left an unfamiliar setting unchanged for the weekend. The presence of a completed training attendance sheet did not demonstrate that every later operator had usable instructions. The proposed next action was to place an updated guide beside the panel, nominate backup staff, and test whether a new operator can follow it. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. A neighbourhood club moved its meetings into the library, increasing evening attendance and equipment use. More electricity during those hours might accompany a larger public service rather than indicate renovation failure. Seat choice also depended on power sockets and proximity to the reference desk, so it was not a temperature measurement. The proposed next action was to move a calibrated sensor through several seating zones and compare occupied hours rather than a single daily reading. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

Field record 26: Service zone 2, seasonal review

During the seasonal review, the team examined ventilation timers in the evening study area. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. The ventilation timer stopped before a newly extended study session ended, and staff opened windows to compensate. Outdoor noise then changed the use of the room, so a simple before-and-after satisfaction score mixed two different effects. The proposed next action was to align the timer with the published timetable, record manual overrides, and check air quality during late sessions. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. Field record 27: Service zone 3, seasonal review

A temporary materials enclosure narrowed the accessible route and forced some visitors to ask staff for assistance. A lower visitor count during that week could reflect the obstruction rather than reduced demand for library services. The proposed next action was to relocate the enclosure, preserve the required route width, and invite accessibility representatives to inspect the change. Field record 28: Service zone 4, seasonal review

During the seasonal review, the team examined solar metering in the roof services area. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. The dashboard displayed imported electricity but omitted locally generated electricity used inside the building. Reading that display alone would confuse lower purchases with lower demand and exaggerate the effect of insulation. The proposed next action was to publish demand and generation as separate series with clear units and preserve the original meter readings. During the seasonal review, the team examined occupancy changes in the community meeting room. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. A neighbourhood club moved its meetings into the library, increasing evening attendance and equipment use. During the seasonal review, the team examined energy dashboards in the public information desk. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. A display placed daily values beside monthly totals without prominent unit labels, leading readers to infer a sudden increase. During the seasonal review, the team examined local business disruption in the delivery entrance. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. Shopkeepers reported missed delivery slots while construction vehicles occupied the shared loading area. The complaint described a real local cost but did not measure the total financial effect on every business in the neighbourhood. The proposed next action was to agree delivery windows in advance, log conflicts, and include affected traders when reviewing the construction schedule. Field record 36: Service zone 12, seasonal review

During the seasonal review, the team examined sensor placement in the archive room. The seasonal review compared the note with weather and opening-hour records from a different month. A temperature sensor was moved beside an internal wall during shelf maintenance and later returned to its original bracket. The resulting discontinuity could be mistaken for improved building performance if the move was omitted from the dataset. The proposed next action was to mark the relocation interval, retain both raw series, and repeat the measurement with documented placement. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.


5. Integrating the records without exaggeration

The records reveal several recurring needs. Reliable operation requires clear responsibilities after the contractor leaves. Energy results must be interpreted alongside service hours and equipment faults. Visitor experience depends on access, noise, and the ability to use the building, not just a comfortable average temperature. Evidence improves when operators preserve original observations, explain corrections, and document where sensors were placed. None of these needs can be reduced to installing a new dashboard.

The three rounds of field visits are not three independent replications of the energy study. Inspectors returned to related locations and often investigated earlier concerns. It would be incorrect to count the thirty-six case notes as thirty-six independent renovated libraries. The pilot still consists of twelve renovated libraries and twelve comparison libraries. Keeping that denominator clear is essential when translating a detailed operational report into a short public summary.

Some recommendations were adopted during the monitoring period, which complicates evaluation. If a ventilation schedule changed halfway through a month, the month does not represent a single stable operating condition. The team retained dates of adjustments and planned sensitivity analyses. It did not discard every difficult month, because doing so would selectively remove the practical problems that expansion would also encounter.

Comparing energy use per visitor can be informative, but the report advises against treating that ratio as a universal target. A quiet room for vulnerable readers may serve an important function without high footfall. A crowded event may have a low energy cost per attendee while creating accessibility problems. Decisions require a statement of the public service being protected. Efficiency is not a substitute for specifying the purpose of a library.

The study also distinguishes electricity from total environmental impact. Materials used in renovation have production and disposal consequences. Solar equipment requires maintenance. Trees need space and water before they mature. The pilot did not measure every lifecycle impact. Its results should not be described as a complete carbon assessment, and the council should not promise a quantified citywide emissions reduction on the basis of these meters alone.

6. Proposed next phase

The next phase should retain a comparison group and, where feasible, use a randomised order of renovation among eligible sites. Eligibility rules should be published before recruitment. Sites with urgent safety needs should receive repairs without being forced into an experiment, but their results should be reported separately. This approach recognises ethical obligations while reducing the risk that enthusiastic volunteers alone determine the apparent success of the programme.

The evaluation team proposes publishing monthly measurements with documented corrections, including missing-data flags and changes in opening hours. Energy demand, electricity purchases, and solar generation should remain separate. A short narrative should accompany unusual periods, such as construction closures or equipment failures. Publication should include unsuccessful interventions and additional maintenance costs, not only photographs of completed work.

Survey recruitment should reach people who stopped visiting as well as current visitors. Accessible formats and varied collection times may improve coverage, but a higher response count does not automatically remove selection bias. The next report should show who was invited, who responded, and which questions were left unanswered. Qualitative interviews can explain barriers that standard scales miss, provided their limits are made explicit.

A lifecycle cost review is also required before a large expansion. The review should include routine maintenance, expected replacement, staff training, and downtime. It should examine what happens when a warranty expires and whether spare parts remain available. The city should not rank proposals by initial purchase cost alone, nor assume that every more expensive option will eventually save money.

The recommendation is a controlled, documented expansion with staged decisions, not an immediate citywide rollout. Before each stage, managers should verify accessible routes, working measurement equipment, a funded maintenance plan, and a clear arrangement for staff support. The council should be willing to stop or revise an intervention when it reduces service quality even if a narrow energy indicator improves.

7. Final conclusion

The pilot offers encouraging observations: purchased electricity fell more in renovated libraries than in the comparison group, many survey respondents preferred the indoor environment, and several operational changes improved daily use. These findings justify further evaluation. They do not prove a universal causal saving, universal public approval, or financial payback.

A responsible summary should preserve the central distinction. Fourteen percent and six percent describe the two observed reductions; the eight-percentage-point difference is not a proven treatment effect. Survey preference concerns respondents, and the spending total does not capture lifecycle costs. Continued comparison, better documentation, representative feedback, and realistic maintenance planning are the next priorities.

END OF ARTICLE


VOLUME 06 OF 12 — REFERENCE COPY

ARTICLE: The Riverhaven Public Buildings Pilot
A fictional evaluation report for reading-comprehension and long-context testing

1. Purpose and decision

Riverhaven is a fictional city whose public buildings face hot summers, unreliable maintenance schedules, and uneven access to quiet indoor space. The city council approved a pilot to renovate public libraries and measure the consequences before expanding the programme. The study concerns electricity, indoor comfort, operating costs, and access. It is not a trial of every environmental policy in the city. All names, dates, institutions, and quantities in this document are invented. The distinction matters because the report describes an evaluation design, not evidence about a real community.

The main intervention combined roof insulation, external shade, improved ventilation controls, and staff training. Some sites also tested solar panels, but solar generation was recorded separately from electricity demand. The council wanted to avoid claiming that a building used less energy merely because a new panel supplied part of the same demand. Tree planting formed a smaller demonstration component outside selected entrances. Newly planted trees did not provide the canopy of mature trees, so the report does not attribute immediate indoor savings to those new trees.

Twelve libraries entered the renovation group. Twelve other libraries were selected as comparison sites on the basis of floor area, opening hours, and the age of the cooling system. Selection was not random. Managers volunteered, and buildings with urgent structural damage were excluded. The two groups therefore cannot be assumed identical. In particular, managers who volunteered may already have been more interested in energy management. That difference could influence electricity use even without physical renovation.

The monitoring period covered twelve months before and twelve months after completion. The primary descriptive result was a fourteen percent reduction in purchased electricity in renovated libraries. The comparison libraries recorded a six percent reduction over the same period. The gap is eight percentage points, not proof of an eight percent causal effect. Weather, attendance, opening hours, equipment replacement, and differences in management all remain possible explanations for part of the gap. The report does not provide a randomised estimate.

2. What the measurements can and cannot show

Monthly meters provide a more reliable total than a visitor survey, but meter data do not explain every change in that total. A broken cooling unit can reduce electricity consumption while making conditions worse. A library that closes early can look efficient while serving fewer people. For that reason, the team retained records of service hours, cancellations, complaints, and occupancy. It also logged equipment faults. Results were reviewed alongside those records rather than ranked by electricity use alone.

Summer savings were larger than winter savings. The report does not convert that pattern into a forecast for every future year. One summer was cooler than the previous summer, and winter attendance changed when several schools altered their timetables. Indoor sensors showed improved afternoon comfort at many renovated sites, but the measurements were incomplete. A sensor placed next to a shaded wall is not representative of the entire reading room. Staff moved some sensors during cleaning; those periods were marked rather than silently treated as comparable.

The survey found that sixty-eight percent of responding visitors preferred the renovated indoor environment. Only forty-one percent of invited visitors completed the questionnaire. Preference among respondents is therefore not equivalent to approval among all residents. People who stopped visiting were especially difficult to reach. The report records this limitation in its main conclusions, not only in an appendix. Comments about quiet space, accessibility, and delayed construction are considered useful explanations, not direct substitutes for representative survey evidence.

The approved budget was twelve million fictional currency units, and recorded expenditure was eleven point four million. That difference is an accounting observation, not a lifecycle benefit calculation. Future maintenance, replacement parts, staff time, and the possible disposal of old equipment have not all been observed. The report does not claim that the project has paid for itself. Several contractors offered warranties with different coverage, making direct comparisons of initial purchase prices misleading.

3. Implementation, access, and mixed outcomes

Construction did not proceed uniformly. Two sites experienced delivery delays that pushed work into examination season. One site kept its main reading room open but temporarily closed a step-free entrance. The team treated that as an accessibility failure requiring correction, even though the electricity meter continued to show lower consumption. A low energy bill cannot compensate for excluding readers who need the accessible entrance.

Staff training helped operators understand timers and ventilation settings, but turnover reduced continuity. A simple guide near the control panel proved useful at several sites. Elsewhere, staff avoided changing unfamiliar settings for fear of damaging equipment. The team therefore recommended a support arrangement with named responsibilities rather than relying on a single training session. Training attendance does not by itself demonstrate that a control system was used correctly in practice.

Residents appreciated quieter streets during periods without construction traffic and liked existing shade near entrances. Shopkeepers reported that temporary barriers disrupted deliveries. Those observations are not contradictory: groups used the area differently and at different times. The evaluation records both. It does not average incompatible experiences into a single unqualified claim that all residents benefited.

Several small interventions did not work as expected. A ventilation schedule at one library conflicted with an evening study session. A demonstration rainwater tank required more cleaning than anticipated. A dashboard made electricity trends visible but confused some users by mixing daily and monthly scales. The report includes these unsuccessful trials because excluding them would overstate how easily the programme could be expanded.

4. How to read the field records

The following records describe repeated monitoring visits across different service zones. A service zone is an administrative grouping for organising visits, not an additional experimental group. Each record is a qualitative case note. The records deliberately include different kinds of evidence: meter observations, staff explanations, visitor accounts, and suggested changes. They are not independent measurements of the citywide reduction reported above.

A district may appear in more than one operational discussion, and the same type of building may appear in several districts. The visits were scheduled to investigate problems, so a high number of critical notes does not establish a population prevalence. Conversely, a positive note does not establish that the same result holds at unvisited sites. The intended reading task is to identify recurring mechanisms and limitations while keeping the overall study design in view.

Inspectors used the existing maintenance log before proposing a causal explanation. Where records were missing, they recorded uncertainty instead of filling the gap with a plausible story. They preferred repairs that preserved public access and could be checked with the available equipment. A proposal became a recommendation for further testing only after someone was assigned to implement it and document the outcome. None of the notes below changes the main statistical conclusion.

Field record 01: Service zone 1, initial inspection

During the initial inspection, the team examined afternoon cooling in the central reading room. The first visit concentrated on describing the situation before changing the operating instructions. Visitors chose seats near the shaded wall, while seats beside the west-facing glass remained less popular. Seat choice also depended on power sockets and proximity to the reference desk, so it was not a temperature measurement. The proposed next action was to move a calibrated sensor through several seating zones and compare occupied hours rather than a single daily reading. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result. A staff explanation was recorded as an explanation to investigate, not as an independently measured cause. This record therefore supports practical follow-up while leaving the overall comparison between renovated and comparison libraries unchanged. The case belongs to the same pilot, not a separate experiment that can be added to the sample size.

A lower visitor count during that week could reflect the obstruction rather than reduced demand for library services. The proposed next action was to relocate the enclosure, preserve the required route width, and invite accessibility representatives to inspect the change. Inspectors asked the site manager to name a responsible person and retain the observation sheet with the maintenance log. They did not treat a proposed remedy as a completed result.
